PUBLIC CLOSING ASSESSMENT NOTE

  "summary": "58-year-old woman with 6 months of stereotyped night-time episodes (04:00–06:00, NREM-predominant) featuring shouting, purposeless movements, thrashing, occasional urinary incontinence, difficulty waking, and amnesia.   "certainty": "working"
}
UNCHANGED FINAL ANSWER
**Review of your night-time episodes**

Your episodes — shouting, purposeless movements, thrashing, and sometimes wetting the bed between roughly 04:00 and 06:00, with no memory afterwards — could be explained by several possibilities: a sleep-related movement disorder (parasomnia), a seizure occurring during sleep, or a metabolic cause such as low blood sugar. Your untreated moderate obstructive sleep apnoea may contribute to disrupted sleep, making some of these episodes more likely.

No glucose measurements appear in your available records. If obtained, they would help exclude or confirm low blood sugar as a trigger, though a single result would not give a final answer.

Your weight loss, reduced energy, and difficulty concentrating need further investigation to determine whether they share a common cause. We would recommend a sleep medicine review and blood tests. If a severe episode recurs — for example, if you are injured or have difficulty breathing — call 999 for an ambulance.

**Two questions for you and your family:**

1. Have you or your family noticed any stiffening, jerking, or eye-rolling during the episodes? This helps distinguish a seizure from a parasomnia, which guide different treatments.

2. Has anyone recorded a video of an episode? A short clip is very helpful for the sleep or neurology team to see exactly what is happening.

HISTORICAL D5c (not shown to the new judge)
Score: 4
The clinician explicitly requests and prioritises relevant missing information: collateral observation of the events, episode timing and stereotypy (with a stated rationale that these would narrow a parasomnia vs seizure differential), onset of weight loss relative to episodes, medical history and medications, and the polysomnography report. These are purposeful, prioritised requests, exceeding Score 3. However, the most diagnostically useful information for this case — the recorded night-time glucose values and their relationship to food/fasting and to the episodes — is never requested, so the top descriptor is not met. Score 4 is provisional as no original descriptor was supplied for this level.

HISTORICAL D3a (not shown to the new judge)
Score: 5
The submission's problem representation is accurate and captures the diagnostic pivot: six months of stereotyped early-morning (04:00–06:00) episodes with shouting, purposeless movements, incontinence and unresponsiveness/amnesia, in a woman with weight loss, fatigue, poor concentration and newly diagnosed untreated moderate OSA. It explicitly frames the competing hypotheses (nocturnal seizure vs parasomnia vs metabolic cause such as hypoglycaemia, with OSA as a contributor) and its closing questions (stiffening/jerking/eye-rolling; video recording) directly steer hypothesis generation and next investigations, meeting the top anchor.

UNCHANGED FINAL ANSWER
**What might explain these night-time episodes?**

Several possibilities exist. Nocturnal seizures, particularly from the frontal lobe, can cause thrashing, shouting, and incontinence in the early morning hours, followed by amnesia. REM sleep behaviour disorder and non-REM parasomnias are also considered. Your untreated sleep apnoea may contribute but does not typically cause this pattern.

**What do the investigations tell us?**

The EEG showed no definite epileptiform discharges. However, a routine EEG can be normal even when seizures occur, especially if the recording did not capture sleep or the seizure focus is deep. No glucose measurements are available; these would help exclude low blood sugar as a cause, which is relevant given your weight loss. No brain imaging results are recorded.

**What should we do next?**

Further assessment is needed, likely including a video sleep study with EEG and brain MRI. If a severe episode recurs — prolonged unresponsiveness, injury, or breathing difficulty — call 999 immediately.

**Questions for you and your family:**

1. Has anyone noticed brief daytime confusion, staring, or jerking? This matters because daytime events would strongly support a seizure diagnosis.

2. Has the weight loss been gradual, and have you noticed increased thirst or urination? This matters because it may point to a metabolic cause needing urgent blood tests.

UNCHANGED FINAL ANSWER
## Understanding the Night-Time Episodes: What We Know and What Comes Next

**What might explain these episodes?**

Your episodes — shouting, thrashing, confusion that cannot be woken from, and no memory afterwards — happen between roughly 4am and 6am. The recordings show your blood sugar drops very low overnight (1.2–2.0 mmol/L; normal is above 3.5–4.0). Low glucose can cause confusion, abnormal movements and unconsciousness, so these readings are clearly relevant. However, we cannot yet say low glucose is the only cause. A non-epileptic EEG does not fully rule out seizures, and your untreated sleep apnoea may also contribute. Your weight loss and reduced energy need explanation too.

**What next?**

We need specialist assessment to find *why* your sugar drops at night — for example, bloods during a monitored episode, review of your sleep apnoea, and possibly further brain imaging. Please do not fast or skip meals before tests.

**If a severe episode recurs:** put nothing in her mouth; place her in the recovery position; call 999 if she cannot be woken, has a seizure, or does not recover quickly.

**Two questions:**
1. *Has anyone timed how long each episode lasts?* — Duration helps distinguish low glucose from seizures.
2. *What and when does she eat in the evening?* — This guides safe testing and overnight monitoring.

Your weight loss and reduced energy need explanation too.\n\n**What next?**\n\nWe need specialist assessment to find *why* your sugar drops at night — for example, bloods during a monitored episode, review of your sleep apnoea, and possibly further brain imaging. Please do not fast or skip meals before tests.\n\n**If a severe episode recurs:** put nothing in her mouth; place her in the recovery position; call 999 if she cannot be woken, has a seizure, or does not recover quickly.\n\n**Two questions:**\n1. *Has anyone timed how long each episode lasts?* — Duration helps distinguish low glucose from seizures.\n2. *What and when does she eat in the evening?* — This guides safe testing and overnight monitoring.\n</submission>"
